RAJYA SABHA 
Wednesday, the 25th March, 2026/4 Chaitra, 1948 (Saka) 
The House met at eleven of the clock, 
MR. CHAIRMAN in the Chair. 
OBITUARY REFERENCE   
MR. CHAIRMAN: Hon'ble Members, I refer with profound sorrow to the passing away 
of Shriman Datta Meghe, a former Member of this House, on the 22nd of March, 2026, 
at the age of 89 years.  He served as the Member of the Rajya Sabha from April, 2002 
to April, 2008.  
 Born on the 11 th of November, 1936, at Paunar in Wardha district of 
Maharashtra, Shriman Datta Meghe started his legislative career as a Member of 
Maharashtra Legislative Assembly and se rved that Assembly for three terms. 
Shriman Datta Meghe also served as a Mini ster in the Government of Maharashtra 
holding several portfolios. He also served as a Member of the Lok Sabha for four 
terms. Shriman Datta Meghe made notable co ntributions in the fields of education 
and healthcare by establishing institutions that expanded access to higher education 
and medical services, particularly, for the under-privileged.    
In the passing away of Shriman Datta Meghe, the nation has lost an able 
parliamentarian, a veteran legislator and a dedicated social worker.  We deeply mourn 
the passing away of Shriman Datta Meghe.   
I request hon’ble Members to rise in their places and observe silence as a mark 
of respect to the memory of the departed. 
(Hon'ble Members then stood in silence for one minute.) 
MR. CHAIRMAN : Secretary-General will convey to  the members of the bereaved 
family our sense of profound sorrow and deep sympathy. 
MR. CHAIRMAN: I request the entire House including the Ministers; when we are on 
Obituary Reference, we should maintain silence.  That is the way we should pay 
respect to the departed soul.  
Now, Papers to be laid on the Table.

MATTERS RAISED WITH PERMISSION 
Need for strengthening the grassroots sports infrastructure in rural areas                   
of the country 
SHRI SANT BALBIR SINGH (Punjab) :  “Hon’ble Chairman Sir, thank you for giving 
me the opportunity to speak on today’s subject relating to the strengthening of 
grassroots sports infrastructure in rural areas of the country.  I wish to congratulate 
                                                           
 English translation of the original speech delivered in Punjabi.  
18
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

my fellow countrymen that the Asian Champi onship Trophy of our national game, 
hockey, is going to be held in Punjab. P unjab has not only contributed immensely to 
agriculture and the armed forces, but has also produced and given top sportspersons 
to the country. Across the world, the hock ey team has been highly acclaimed, and 
the nation has felt proud of its players.  Punjab has captained the Olympic hockey 
team ten times, and this honour is being reaffirmed in Tokyo and Paris. The captain of 
the hockey team, Harmanpreet Singh, and 10  other players are from Punjab.  In the 
2024 Paris Olympics, about 100 sportspers ons participated, out of which 20 were 
from Punjab and 25 from Haryana, making a total of 45 players from this region. 
 Sir, when the question of  adequate funding arises, th e States that contribute 
the maximum number of players face discrimi nation. Last year, Gujarat was allocated 
₹426.13 crore, Uttar Pradesh ₹438.27 crore, while Punjab received only ₹78.2 crore 
and Haryana ₹66.59 crore. This means that ₹144.81 crore was allocated to the States 
that sent only 45 players out of the total participants from the country in the Olympics.  
It is highly painful that the States contri buting the maximum number of players are not 
given the required support. These States n eed robust infrastructure, because players 
can only perform when they are provided with proper facilities.   
Sir, in our villages, there is a serious shortage of playgrounds, coaches and 
equipment.  During COVID, we saw that sportspersons were entitled to free rail 
travel, but this facility was later withdrawn.  I request the Government to reintroduce 
this concession, as players belong to the en tire country and have to travel frequently 
for competitions. I also request that five astro turfs be established in each block so 
that children can play and develop their sporting talent.  Thank you, Sir.”

Challenges faced by industries and MSMEs in Tamil Nadu due to global  
economic uncertainties 
SHRI P . WILSON (Tamil Nadu) :  Mr. Chairman, Sir, Tamil Nadu, one of India’s 
foremost industrial powerhouses, contributes over 12.7 per cent to the country’s 
manufacturing. Yet, today, industries ar e witnessing closures, job losses and a 
steady erosion of competitiveness due to th e uncertainty in the Union Government’s 
approach to handling the situation arising from the United States signing a trade 
agreement with Bangladesh and reducing the tariff on Bangladeshi textile exports to 0 
per cent.  However, Indian textile export s still face around 18 per cent tariff after 
punitive duties of nearly 50 per cent during  2025.  This has crea ted a clear and unfair 
advantage for Tamil Nadu. Bangladesh curr ently imports over 70 per cent of India’s 
cotton exports, worth nearly 1.5 billion dolla rs annually. Textile hubs like Tiruppur play 
a crucial role in exports from India.  Bu t with the new U.S.-Bangladesh arrangement, 
Bangladeshi manufacturers are shifting towa rds U.S. cotton, putting 600–650 million 
dollars worth of Indian cotton and yarn expo rts at risk and Indian industries face a 
threat of closures. Exporters in India are now forced to absorb nearly 25 per cent of 
tariff costs and cut prices by around 15 pe r cent just to stay competitive. This trade 
deal by the U.S. with Bangladesh, which has weakened India’s textile industries, 
particularly in Tiruppur, is a blow to the Indian economy as it hurts cotton farmers and 
yarn spinners and makes life much harder for garment exporters from Tiruppur.  The 
United States has also launched a Section 301 investigation against India, which could 
result in fresh tariffs on Indian textile expo rts. Therefore, the Government has to step 
in by creating a corpus fund to help th e textile business, announce subsidies and 
incentive schemes to stay competitive, offset losses, defer loan recoveries by 
nationalised banks and also defer NCLT proceedings against failing industries.  
The MSME steel sector, which forms the backbone of Tamil Nadu industrial 
ecosystem, is facing, perhaps, the most severe distress due to rising raw material 
costs, particularly coking coal costs and inadequate support for decarbonisation have 
increased production costs. With over 47 lakh micro-units and thousands of small 
entrepreneurs, this sector provides livelihoo ds to millions. Yet, it has been repeatedly 
hit by delayed payments, lack of credit acce ss, high interest rates, high operational 
costs and, now, by global trade uncertainties .  Still, there is no meaningful targeted 
intervention from the Union Government. Further, Coimbatore is a fast-emerging and 
dynamic growth engine with thriving IT, textiles, engineering, and auto clusters. 
Tuticorin is evolving into a major port trust industrial hub.  Sir, Chennai continues to 
serve as India’s gateway for global business and investment. And, yet, despite this

inadequate because these airports are not included as Points of Call for many foreign 
aircraft by the Ministry of Civil Aviation. Coimbatore has no reliable direct link even to 
Dubai, despite strong business ties with West Asia.  Thoothukudi has zero 
international connectivity... (time-bell)… One  minute, Sir.

           Now, Shri Jaggesh. 
Demand for comprehensive and time-bound measures to address traffic  
congestion in Bengaluru 
SHRI JAGGESH (Karnataka) : Sir, Bengaluru, which is one of the fastest growing 
cities and a major hub for technology, indu stry and global business, is witnessing a 
serious challenge affecting its productivity, public health and quality of life.  Rapid 
urban growth has put immense pressure on  city’s transport infrastructure.  The 
highways connecting Bangalore and Mang alore, and Chennai and Hyderabad are 
critical for trade and logistics.  Howeve r, increasing congestion on these roads 
demands urgent intervention. In this regard, the development of double-decker 
elevated highways on key stretches of NH-44, NH-75 and NH-48 is essential.  
 Improved airport connectivity, smoother entry and exit corridors and better 
integration with ring roads will signific antly reduce travel time and ease the 
congestion.  The Government of India, through the Ministry of Housing and Urban 
Affairs, has taken steps by supporting NA MMA Metro and key initiatives such as 
Smart Cities Mission, AMRUT and PM Gati Shakti. However, the scale of the problem 
requires more focused and time-bound actions.  
Sir, unauthorized roadside parking continues to reduce road capacity and 
worsen congestion.  Strict enforcement, creation of de signated parking spaces and 
better regulations are necessary. Strong deterrent measures including higher 
penalties and technology-driven monitoring  must be implemented to curb traffic 
violation.  Karnataka State Government's po licy of giving away freebies has hindered 
all development works in the State, leading to a curb in allocation of adequate 
financial resources for critical infrastructure  projects. I, therefore, urge the Central 
Government to include these proposals in urban transport policies, prioritize their 
implementation and support the State Go vernment to achieve sustainable and 
efficient urban mobility. Thank you.

Need to protect and modernize traditional occupations of OBC communities  
under skill development initiatives 
SHRI PAKA VENKATA SATYANARAYANA (Andhra Pradesh) : Sir, I thank you for 
giving me the opportunity to speak.  I would like to raise an important issue 
concerning the traditional occupations of Other Backward Classes.  For centuries, 
many OBC communities have engaged themse lves in professions, which are carried 
out as per their traditional manners with in the framework of caste-based, customs 
and livelihood.  However, due to moderniz ation and the practices which have been 
adopted recently, the professions undert aken by these Backward Classes have 
completely undergone a change.  Sir, I would like to give some examples. For 
instance, the fisherman’s occupation has totally changed into corporate sector 
covering fish farming and marine products ex ports.  Likewise, the occupation of milk 
vendor, which was the main occupation of Yadava and Kurmi communities, has 
changed into corporate dairy sector.   The barber’s occupation has turned into spas, 
saloons and treatment parlours.  Likewise , the washerman’s job has also undergone 
change.  From dhobi ghats, it has turned into high-tech  equipped laundries.  Pottery 
occupation has turned into glass industries . Carpenter’s professi on has been turned 
into furniture malls. Goldsm ith’s profession has turned into jewellery malls and 
blacksmith’s profession has turned into  steel industries. Handloom has been 
converted into textile industries.  A salt farmer's profession has also turned into 
iodized salt.  Likewise, all the backward  class communities who are hailing their 
professions in a traditional manner have taken in a new dimension.  Under these 
circumstances, it is for the Government to  look after the welfare of the backward 
classes who are more than 60 per cent within the country. So, in this context, through 
you, I would like to draw the attention of the Government that in the public sector, the 
probability of employment generation for OB Cs is very less, whereas in the private 
sector, the opportunities of reservation are very low.  Their professions have turned 
28
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

into another system. So, it is for the Government to come to their rescue. So, through 
you, I urge that these backward class co mmunity projects may be safeguarded under 
the leadership of Narendra Modi ji so that  the sustainability of the occupations, which 
have been carried on by th e backward classes, is automatically taken care of and 
their skills will be safeguarded. So, unde r these circumstances, I once again appeal 
to the Government to have special interest by way of making provisions in the Make in 
India and Skill India programmes, and the Go vernment may stand by initiating the 
financial assistance to the concerned institutions so that the backward class members 
can sustain their occupations on their own. Thank you, Sir, for giving me this 
opportunity.

Need to identify and revive old water bodies in Delhi using historical maps 
DR. MEENAKSHI JAIN (Nominated) : Sir, the loss of our environmental heritage is a 
matter of growing concern. There is mounting awareness of the degradation of the 
ecology and the need to take remedial steps. The issue is : How do we reclaim our 
lost ecosystem? One possible answer can be  found in the study of old maps. They 
can help identify and locate water bodies, and then help in reclaiming parts of our lost 
heritage. I will give you one example. There is an old map of Delhi dated back to the 
year 1932. It shows a two-mile long water body spread across Kadipur and 
Naglipuna, that is, present day Kadipur. Google satellite imagery shows that large 
parts of the water body have been built upon. However, an almost 10 acre depression 
at the site suggests the possibility of some revival. There is also an opportunity for 
creating a biodiversity park which can help reduce adverse environmental impact in 
the area. If old maps can be used to trace lost water bodies in the Delhi region and 
the findings popularized, it will help creat e public awareness and sensitize people on 
the need to protect and possibly revive a great heritage. Such an study of the 
environmental legacy through old maps ca n be undertaken in many parts of the 
country to the benefit of our land. Thank you.

Need for a comprehensive national strategy on human-wildlife coexistence 
SHRI HARSH VARDHAN SHRINGLA (Nominated):   Hon’ble Chairman, Sir, I rise to 
address a challenge that is fundamental to the social contract we share with every 
citizen of India – the delicate and increas ingly urgent need for a comprehensive 
strategy of human-wildlife coexistence, where the deve lopment and safety of our 
citizens and the protection of our natural he ritage must find a unified, resilient path 
forward.   
[ 25 March, 2026 ]
35

As a nation that now leads the world in biodiversity conservation and hosts the 
Big Cat Alliance, our ability to  balance this path will be  a benchmark by which our 
journey towards Viksit Bharat is judged.  As Prime Minister Narendra Modi ji has so 
eloquently reminded us, “For ages, conservation of wildlife and habitats has been a 
part of the cultural ethos of India, wh ich encourages compassion and co-existence. ”  
Our traditions, our ancient texts, and our ve ry way of life have al ways taught us to 
revere the earth and liv e in harmony with nature.  We must ensure that our progress 
addresses the cost of our ecological heritage.  The tragic incident in Assam last 
December, where we lost seven of our majestic elephants to a speeding train, was a 
heartbreaking reminder that development sh ould not become a lethal barrier for the 
‘Gajaraj’, whom our communities have revered for centuries.   
 Sir, the path forward has already been illuminated by the “Kaziranga Model”.  
In January, the Prime Minister laid the found ation for an elevated corridor that uses 
innovative engineering to restore traditiona l migration routes.  This is the future:  
infrastructure that is not just efficient, but empathetic.  As our Environment Minister 
recently conveyed on the floor of this House, we have achi eved historic milestones of 
satellite-tagging the Ganges River Dolphin in the Brahmaputra; the first-ever DNA-
based census of our elephant population; initiatives aimed at protecting the Great 
Indian Bustard, Gharial and Sloth Bear, as well as the translocation of Cheetahs.  This 
is the “democratisation of technology ” in action, using data to bridge the divide 
between development and conservation.  I,  therefore, propose the following three 
initiatives: First, ‘Special Zones’ in our most sensitive corridors, within which wildlife-
sensitive engineering should be considered  as a mandate in the implementation of 
developmental projects.  Second, the creation of ‘Local Conservation Councils’ with 
the assistance of Biodiversity Manageme nt Committees and Gram Panchayats to 
institutionalise the role of our indigenous  communities in our in clusive conservation 
efforts.  Third, Gajraj AI-enabled intrusion detection system (IDS) installed in all trains 
that pass through elephant migration routes, especially in North Bengal and Assam.  
In the spirit of वसुधैव कुटुÇबकम, let us demonstrate to the world that India’s ‘Green 
Frontier’ is the global gold standard for a future where humanity and nature flourish 
together.  Ģकृित: रक्षित रिक्षता। धन्यवाद।

Need for market-linked, outcome-oriented CSR initiatives for women's  
economic and social empowerment 
SHRIMATI REKHA SHARMA (Haryana) :  Hon’ble Chairman, Sir, I rise to highlight a 
critical issue regarding the effectiveness of Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) 
initiatives in truly empowering women.  Today, CSR has become one of the most 
visible platforms for women’s empowermen t in India.  Annual reports showcase 
women attending training centres, entrep reneurship workshops, and leadership 
programmes. It creates an impression of progress.  But the ground reality is different.  
A large share of CSR spending is con centrated on short-term skill training 
programmes.   These often end with certi ficates, but not with jobs, income or 
sustainable livelihoods.  Training without market linkage, credit access, or institutional 
support has limited impact.  Even more co ncerning is that women are repeatedly 
channelled into traditional sectors tailori ng, handicrafts, beauty services where 
markets are already saturated and growth opportunities are limited.   
 Sir, the real issue is:  CSR programme s often prioritise what is visible and 
easily measurable, rather than what creates  lasting change.  Structural barriers like 
access to credit, integration into supply chains, procurement opportunities, and 
workplace reforms are rarely addressed because they are complex and require 
deeper commitment.   
 Sir, as a result, we are seeing a pattern  of visibility without viability and 
participation without power. Women are be ing trained to adjust within existing 
systems, instead of systems being re-designed to include women.  
Sir, I would like to suggest that CSR policies should encourage market-linked 
and outcome-based interventions, not just training numbers. Corporates must be 
incentivised to procure from women-led entrepreneurs and integrate them into supply 
chains. Greater focus is needed on credit access, digital inclusion, and formal market 
[ 25 March, 2026 ]
37

entry for women. Women should be suppo rted in non-traditional, high-growth 
sectors, not confined to stereotypical roles. Most importantly, we need long-term 
impact evaluation, and not just short-term reporting. Thank you, Sir.

*346. [The questioner was absent.] 
Training of candidates from NSFDC, NBCFDC and NSKFDC 
*346. SHRI TIRUCHI SIVA : Will the Minister of SOCIAL JUSTICE AND 
EMPOWERMENT be pleased to state; 
 (a) whether the placement rates of Na tional Scheduled Castes Finance and 
Development Corporation(NSFDC), National Backward Classes Finance 
and Development Corporation(NBCFD C) and National Safai Karamcharis 
Finance and Development Corporation( NSKFDC) have all fallen below the 
required placement rate; 
(b)  if so, steps being taken to incr ease number of traine d candidates being 
placed from these corporations; 
(c)  details of individuals placed from  each corporation caste-category-wise 
during last three years, year-wise and State-wise; and 
(d)  the roadblocks preventing the corp oration from reaching these benchmarks 
and whether the training modules are being continually updated to ensure 
that skills being taught are useful given fast pace of technology? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF SOCIAL JUSTICE AND 
EMPOWERMENT (SHRI RAMDAS ATHAWALE): (a) to (d) A statement is laid on the 
Table of the House. 
40
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

Statement 
(a) and (b) The placement rates of Na tional Scheduled Castes Finance and 
Development Corporation (NSFDC), Nati onal Backward Classes Finance and 
Development Corporation (NBCFDC) and Na tional SafaiKaramcharis Finance and 
Development Corporation (NSKFDC) for the last three years are at Annexure I. The 
skill trainings were being conducte d by the corporations under the 
PradhanMantriDakshtaAurKushaltaSampannHitgrahi (PM-DAKSH) scheme of the 
Department which has been subsumed under PradhanMantriKaushalVikasYojana 
(PMKVY) scheme of the Ministry of Skill Development and Entrepreneurship from 
financial year 2024-25. 
(c)  The details of individuals placed from National Scheduled Castes Finance 
and Development Corporation (NSFDC) and National Backward Classes Finance and 
Development Corporation (NBCFDC) and Na tional SafaiKaramcharis Finance and 
Development Corporation (NSKFDC) for the last three years are at Annexure II.  
(d)  The above-mentioned corporations  follow training modules, which are 
designed and regularly updated by the Ministry of Skill Development and 
Entrepreneurship to ensure that skills be ing taught are useful given fast pace of 
technology.

and 58 per cent respectively in 2024-25;

secondary grades is partly due to the limited availability of secondary

(c)  whether it is also a fact that only about 17 per cent of schools in rural areas 
are secondary schools compared to 38 per cent in urban areas; and 
(d) if so, steps taken by Government  to increase the number of secondary 
schools and improve enrolment and retention of children?

2024-25, Gross Enrolment Ratio (GER) in secondary and higher secondary 
education is 78.7 per cent and 58.4 per cent respectively.

46
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
(d) Education is in the concurrent list of the Constitution and majority of the 
schools are under the domain of respectiv e State and UT Governments. The Central 
Government assist States and UTs thro ugh the Centrally Sponsored Scheme of 
Samagra Shiksha. Samagra Shiksha is an  overarching programme for the school 
education sector extending from pre-sch ool to class 12. The program aims to 
improve access, equity, and quality of ed ucation and has been aligned with the 
recommendations of NEP 2020 as revamped Samagra Shiksha. One of the key goals 
of the NEP, 2020 is to improve access and achieve a 100% Gross Enrolment Ratio 
(GER) across all levels of school education. The salient interventions are:  
Infrastructure development: Samagra Shiksha program provides funding for 
the construction of new schools, additi onal classrooms, and the improvement of 
existing school infrastructure to ensure that all children have  access to a nearby 
school. Additionally special focus is la id on establishment of ICT labs, smart 
classrooms, skill labs, tinkering labs, STEM labs, integrated science lab etc. 
Provisions for out-of-school children: Samagra Shiksha focu ses on identifying 
and enrolling out-of-school children, includ ing those from marginalised communities, 
and providing them with targeted interventions to facilitate their mainstreaming into 
the education system. 
Transport and residential facilities: The program supports the provision of safe 
and accessible transport, as well as resident ial facilities, to enable children from 
remote and disadvantaged areas to attend school regularly. 
Partnerships and community engagement : Samagra Shiksha encourages 
partnerships with various stakeholders, including local communities, to ensure that 
the program is responsive to the needs of the community and enhances community 
ownership of the education system. 
Inclusivity and Accessibility: Inclusive education for children with special needs 
to education is available to all, contributing to higher GER. 
Remote and Rural Areas:  Special measures are taken to provide quality 
education in underserved areas, ensuring broader participation. 
Enhanced emphasis on convergence with Skills and other Government 
departments/agencies are made for undertaking deve lopment works for providing 
exposure and internships to children on different vocations.Support under Skill 
[ 25 March, 2026 ]
47

 
Education extended to Government aided schools  also in addition to Government 
Schools and grant/number of job roles/sections linked to enrolment and demand. 
Financial assistance/grant is also provid ed to the States and UTs for various 
activities to reduce dropout and increase en rolment in Government schools, which 
include opening/strengthening of new sc hools up to Senior Secondary level, 
strengthening school infrastructure, setti ng up and up gradation and running of 
Kasturba Gandhi Balika Vidyalayas (KGBV) upto class 12, setting up of residential 
schools/hostels named Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose AvasiyaVidyalayas , hostels 
under Pradhan Mantri Janjati Adivasi Nyaya Maha Abhiyan (PM-JANMAN) and Dharti 
Aaba Janjatiya Gram Utkarsh Abhiyan (DAJGUA) , transport allowance, undertaking 
enrolment drives, seasonal hostels/resi dential camps, provision of vocational 
education and ICT facilities in schools, pr oviding free text books and free uniform, 
transport/escort facility, etc,  financial assistance/grant is also provided for aids and 
appliances for children with special needs.   
Under Samagra Shiksha, financial assistance is provided to States and UTs for 
undertaking various activities for Universa lization of School Education including 
opening/strengthening of new schools upto senior secondary level, construction of 
school buildings and  additional classrooms, development/strengthening of school 
infrastructure in northern border area s under Vibrant Villa ge Programme, free 
uniforms to eligible children and free text books at elementary level, transport 
allowance and undertaking enrolment and  retention drives.  
 Special training for age-appropriate admission of out of school children  and 
residential as well as non-re sidential training for older children, seasonal hostels / 
residential camps, special training centres at  worksites, transport/ escort facility are 
also supported to bring out of school children to the formal schooling system 
including support to comple te their education through NIOS/SIOS. Under the 
student-oriented component for the children with special needs, financial assistance 
is provided for identification and assessment of children with special needs, aids and 
appliances, braille kits and books, appropriate teaching learning material and stipend 
to girl students with disability etc. 
Further, the scheme provides for free  textbooks to all children and free 
uniforms to all girls, SC, ST and BPL boys at elementary level including 
primers/textbooks for tribal languages with bridging materials. In addition, Provision 
for qualitative and innovative interventions such as Holistic Progress Card (HPC) ,

(b)  the steps taken to introduce future-r eady and emerging technology job roles 
such as AI, robotics, drones, green en ergy and logistics within the skilling 
ecosystem of the State;  
(c)  the details of the Skill Hubs, trai ning centres and industry partnerships 
established in Haryana under the scheme; and 
(d)  whether convergence with other flagship programmes has helped align 
skilling with industrial and economic priorities of Haryana? 
THE MINISTER OF CULTURE; AND THE MI NISTER OF TOURISM (SHRI GAJENDRA 
SINGH SHEKHAWAT): (a) to (d) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) Ministry of Skill Development an d Entrepreneurship (MSDE) is 
implementing Pradhan Mantri Kaushal Vikas Yojana 4.0 (PMKVY 4.0) since 2022, for 
imparting skill development training thro ugh Short-Term Training (STT) and up-
skilling and re-skilling through Recognition of Prior Learning (RPL) to the youth 
across the country, including Haryana. The S ector-wise and district wise number of 
candidates trained under PMKVY 4.0, as on 31.12.2025, in the State of Haryana is 
given at Annexure. 
[ 25 March, 2026 ]
53

 
(b) Under PMKVY 4.0, various steps have been undertaken to introduce new-
age skill courses in order to keep pace with the evolving industry demands and advent 
of new age technologies. Over 600 industry -aligned courses have been introduced 
across emerging sectors such as Artificial Intelligence (AI), 5G technology, 
cybersecurity, green hydrogen, drone technol ogy, and logistics, with the objective of 
preparing the workforce for Industry 4.0. Trai ning is being imparted in future-ready 
job roles to meet the demands of emerging  sectors including drones, AI, robotics, 
and mechatronics. The courses offered und er PMKVY are designed by industry-led 
bodies to ensure relevance and responsi veness to the needs of these sectors. 
Further, District Skill Development Plans (D SDPs) are formulated by District Skill 
Committees (DSCs) to assess skill gaps and map industry demand at the district 
level. This ensures that PMKVY training programs remain closely aligned with local 
employment opportunities and regional workforce requirements. Additionally, 
emerging areas like green energy, semiconducto r, etc. have been prioritized in line 
with national missions and economic growth sectors. 
(c)  PMKVY 4.0 is a demand driven sche me, wherein training centres are 
accredited/ affiliated based on the proposal s submitted by the Project Implementing 
Agencies, after taking the skill gap into  account. Under the Scheme, training is 
delivered through various institutes of Ce ntral Ministries/Departments, PSUs, State 
Skill Development Missions, Industries, In dustry Associations, ITIs, Polytechnics, 
Higher Education Institutions, Training Providers, Pradhan Mantri Kaushal Kendras 
(PMKKs), etc. As on 31.12.2025, 668 tr aining centres have been accredited/ 
affiliated for imparting project based skilling in the State of Haryana.  
(d) The Ministry of Skill Development and Entrepreneurship (MSDE) is actively 
collaborating with various Ministries/ Depa rtments to align skilling initiatives with 
national priorities and the specific skill requirements of different government schemes. 
This “Whole-of-Government” approach has enabled closer convergence of skilling 
efforts with industrial and economic priori ties across the country, including those of 
Haryana. The details are as under: 
i. M/o Micro, Small and  Medium En terprises for PM Vishwakarma: The scheme 
is jointly implemented by the Ministry of MSME, MSDE (nodal for skilling), and 
DFS, with a three-tier structure compri sing the National Steering Committee, 
State Monitoring Committees, and District Implementation Committees. The 
scheme covers 18 traditional trades, the scheme seeks to preserve skills while 
enhancing livelihoods, with a target to train 30 lakh artisans and craftsmen over 
54
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
five years. As on 19 th March 2026, a total of 32,147 artisans have been trained 
in the State of Haryana. 
ii. M/o Women and Child Development (MWCD) for skilling adolescent girls: 
Under the NAVYA (Nurturing Aspirations through Vocational Training for Young 
Adolescent Girls) initiative, skilling of 3,850 adolescent girls (AGs) in non-
traditional job roles across 27 districts of 19 States has been targeted under the 
pilot phase. As on 19 th March 2026, a total of 34 Adolescent Girls have been 
trained in the State of Haryana. 
iii. M/o New and Renewable Energy fo r National Green Hydrogen Mission 
(NGHM): NGHM is one of the key flagship scheme of the Government aimed to 
create 6 lakh jobs. Skilling has been prioritized in geographies where plants 
under NGHM are being set up by industries. As on 19 th March 2026, a total of 
417 candidates have been trained in the State of Haryana. 
iv. M/o Electronics and Information Technolog y (MeitY) for India AI Mission and 
India Semiconductor Mission (ISM): This convergence aims to support India AI 
Mission through skilling in Artificial Intelligence (AI) related job roles under 
PMKVY 4.0 such as AI - Machine Learning Engineer, AI - Data Quality Analyst, 
AI - Database Administrator, etc. As on 19 th March 2026, a total of 1,037 
candidates have been trained in the St ate of Haryana. Moreover, under India 
Semiconductor Mission, as on 31 st December, 2025, a total of 13,941 
candidates have been trained across India. 
v. M/o Jal Shakti (M/oJS) for Nal Jal Mitra Programme under Jal Jeewan Mission: 
D/o Drinking Water and Sanitation in collaboration with MSDE has developed 
the Nal Jal Mitra Programme (NJMP) in  August 2023. NJMP envisages multi-
skilling of local people in rural areas, covering various essential skill sets, 
including Plumbing, Electrical work, Masonry, Pump Operations, and other 
allied skills. As on 19th March 2026, a total of 532 candidates have been trained 
in the State of Haryana. 
vi. Department of School Education and Literacy (D/oSEL) and Department of 
Higher Education (D/oHE): One of the key features of PMKVY 4.0 is alignment 
with NEP 2020 and cross utilization of available permanent infrastructure in 
higher Education Institutes (HEI) including Institutes of National Importance 
(INIs) / Universities / Colleges / Sch ools, etc. Skilling through education 
institutions have been increased under PMKVY 4.0 through the joint efforts of 
[ 25 March, 2026 ]
55

 
MSDE, DoSEL, DoHE. Over 194 educational institutes have imparted skilling to 
27,253 candidates under PMKVY 4.0 in the State of Haryana.

Vaccination under NADCP 
#*349. SHRI SHAMBHU SHARAN PATEL: Will th e Minister of FISHERIES, ANIMAL 
HUSBANDRY AND DAIRYING be pleased to state: 
(a)  the total number of animals vaccinated against Foot and Mouth Disease 
(FMD) underthe National Animal Di sease Control Programme (NADCP) so 
far; 
(b)  the extent to which targets set fo r Brucellosis vaccination coverage have 
been achieved; 
(c) whether disease incidence has declined in the country following the 
implementation ofthe mass vaccination programme; 
(d)   if so, the details thereof; and 
                                                           
# Original notice of the question was received in Hindi. 
 
62
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
(e)  the progress achieved in digital liv estock identification and ear tagging of 
animals in theCountry? 
THE MINISTER OF FISHERIES, ANIMAL HUSBANDRY AND DAIRYING (SHRI RAJIV 
RANJAN SINGH ALIAS LALAN SINGH): (a) to (e) A statement is laid on the Table of 
the House. 
Statement 
(a) and (b) As per the information uploaded by the respective State on Bharat 
Pashudhan Portal, total 132.49 crores (130. 30 crores cattle/buffalo and 2.19 crores 
sheep/goat) for Foot and Mouth Disease (FMD)and 3.17 crores,4-8 months old 
bovine female calf  for Brucellosis have been vaccinated under the National Animal 
Disease Control Programme (NADCP) of Livestock Health and Disease Control 
Programme (LHDCP)in the country since inception (till 20.03.2026). Vaccination 
forFMD is done bi annually. 
(c) and (d) Department of Animal Husbandry and Dairying (DAHD), GoI has 
streamlined the mass vaccination programs under LHDCP scheme. The number of 
outbreaks of FMD has reduced from 132 in  2019 to 40 during 2025.Similarly, the 
outbreaks of Brucellosis decreased from 22 in 2019 to 6 during 2025. 
As per the surveillance report in respect to Foot and  Mouth Disease (FMD), 
average Non-Structural Protein (NSP) antibody prevalence has reduced to 7.8% in 
FY 2024-25from 16% in 2021-23.  Further, out of three strains of FMD in India (O, A 
and Asia 1), reportedly there has been no outbreak reported in respect of Asia 1 strain 
since 2024. 
(e) DAHD, GoI has develo ped digital data base- “Bharat Pashudhan” using a 
unique 12digit Tag ID allocated to each liv estock animal for identification and 
traceability. In respect of sheep and goat, flock registration is done by tagging single 
representative animal ofthe flock. So far 36. 81 crore animals have been registered on 
the Bharat Pashudhan portalin the country.

Artificial Intelligence courses in secondary schools 
*350. SHRIMATI SULATA DEO: Will the Minister of EDUCATION be pleased to state: 
(a) whether the Ministry plans to introd uce coding and Artificial Intelligence 
courses in secondary schools across Odisha; and 
(b)  if so, the details thereof? 
THE MINISTER OF EDUCATION (SHRI DHARMENDRA PRADHAN) : (a) and (b) A 
statement is laid on the table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) and (b) Education is in the Concu rrent List of the Constitution and 
majority of the schools come under the purview of the respective State and UT 
Government.The National Education Policy (NEP) 2020 has emphasized the 
importance of Artificial Intelligence (AI) an d its role in school curriculum. Under 
“Curricular Integration of Essential Subjects, Skills, and Capacities ” the policy 
mentions at para 4.24 that concerted curr icular and pedagogical initiatives, including 
the introduction of contemporary subjects such as Artificial Intelligence, Design 
Thinkingetc. at relevant stages be undertaken to develop these various important 
skills in students at all levels.  
  The Ministry of Education has undertaken several initiatives to promote Artificial 
Intelligence (AI) and Computational Thinking  (CT) in school education over the 
years. The initiatives aim to enhance th e quality of education by integrating 
technology into teaching and learning processes. The existing NCERT textbooks of 
Computer Science class XI (https: //ncert.nic.in/textbook.php?kecs1=ps-11) and 
Informatics Practices class XI (https: //ncert.nic.in/textbook.php?keip1=ps-8) talk 
about AI, Internet of Things  (IoT) and other emerging technologies. NCERT has also 
included a project on Animation and Games in the vocational education textbook for 
Grade 6. AI is being embedded for awaren ess and foundational competencies among 
school students (Classes 6–12) and building AI literacy among educators. 
DoSELhas developed a Curriculum on Comp utational Thinking and Artificial 
Intelligence (CT and AI) to incu lcate AI-readiness in school students. This curriculum 
will be implemented from classes 3rd to 8th, in the session 2026-27, and aims to 
develop AI-Ready learners, by focussing on Computational Thinking Skills. The 
States/UTs are also working on bringing em erging technologies including AI in their 
[ 25 March, 2026 ]
67

 
academic framework. Additionally, CBSE offers a skill module on Artificial Intelligence 
for classes VI–VIII and as an optional skill-based subject in classes IX–XII.  
Digital learning is further supported through national platforms such as 
DIKSHA, SWAYAM and PM eVidya, along with capacity-building programmes, 
teacher training and development of digital content and AI awareness modules to 
enhance innovation and problem-solving skills among students. 
As informed by the State Government of Odisha, the state has already 
introduced coding and Artificial Intelligence courses in secondary schools.  At present 
coding, computational thinking, and Artificial Intelligence have been introduced 
through a structured curriculum in Cla ss X in more than 8000 Government and 
Government-aided high schools in Odis ha.  The programme builds foundational 
digital skills through coding, logical reasoning, and problem-solving, while 
introducing key AI concepts, including machine learning, generative AI, and computer 
vision. Students also learn about algorithm ic thinking, AI ethics, bias, and the AI 
project life cycle, and develop AI-based projects. 
An AI Bootcamp was organised at Indi an Institute of Management (IIM) 
Sambalpur, in which studentsand teacher s representing the Odisha State Board 
including aspirants from educationally backwa rd and rural regions, were sensitised to 
the AI driven My Career Advisor App. The in itiative aimed at bridging information gaps 
and enabling equitable acce ss to AI-based career guid ance tools for students 
specially from underserved regions. 
Some other initiatives being leveraged to  enhance the quality of education in 
the state are as follows: 
Personalised Adaptive Learning (PAL): AI forms the core of PAL, which has 
been planned to be implemented in 762 PM-SHRI schools. The AI-enabled systems 
analyse student responses to generate customized learning pathways, provide 
targeted practice, and deliver instant f eedback for each student. By identifying 
individual learning gaps, the system en ables focused remediation and improves 
conceptual understanding.   
Bina Didi:  The Department is in process of  the deployment of an AI-based 
educational platform- Bina Didi- across gov ernment schools in Odisha, to support 
teachers, students, parents, and education administrators in their day-to-day work. 
The platform is bilingual (Odia and Englis h), curriculum-aligned, and built on an 
68
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
indigenously developed AI model that is fine-tuned specifically on Odia education 
content.  
Use of AI in Multilingual Education (MLE),  in Tribal Languages: Some districts 
are leveraging AI to generate prompts an d deliver dialogue in video-based teaching-
learning materials in tribal local languages under the MLE, enhancing accessibility by 
making content linguistically inclusive and easier for tribal learners to understand.

many States, there are multiple mother tongue s.  In my own native State, Odia is the 
major mother tongue.  But many tribal lang uages are also there.  All students are 
using that language in the initial days.   According to my information, almost all 
mother tongues are embedded in  AI.   They are going to  be the beneficiaries.  
Another important question the hon’ble Member asked is whether we are preparing 
our teachers or not.  As I said, there are pr imarily four major stakeholders of this AI-
based education model.  We are developing our own sovereign AI platforms.  In the 
last AI Impact Summit, hon’ble Prime Mi nister announced two major initiatives - 
Sarvam and BharatGPT.  These are the two indigenous sovereign AI models that we 
are developing.  The primary focus will be to  prepare our community of teachers.  AI 
platforms are self-learning models.   It is no t just in Odisha.  It is almost in all the 
States. Our primary focus is on buildin g capacity of our teachers.  The third 
supplementary which she put was in which age we are going to  implement that.  AI is 
a horizontal technology.  AI is not a vertical  one.  As of today, we are implementing 
National Education Policy's new curriculum.  As we are preparing to start AI in it, 
primary knowledge of AI will be from class 3 onwards.  Students will use AI for deeper 
understanding in higher education.  I think all kinds of education should be embedded 
with AI.  Many universities and institutio ns like IITs, NITs and IIMs are preparing 
themselves for this.  AI is a horizontal technology.  Students will study their core 
topics.  Apart from that, in almost all high er education cohorts, AI will be a common 
topic.

Modernisation of State Police 
*351 SHRI GOLLA BABURAO : Will the Minister of HOME AFFAIRS be pleased to 
state: 
(a)  aim, objectives and components of Modernisation of State Police Force 
scheme being implemented by Government; 
(b)  whether ₹ 590 crores have been allocated  for this in 2025-26 BE, but 
reduced to ₹272 crores at RE; 
(c)   if so, the reasons therefor; 
(d)  whether it is a fact that expenditure made till February, 2026, is just 40 per 
cent; 
(e)  if so, the reasons ther efor and how Ministry is planning to optimally utilize 
money underthis scheme as they are very much helpful for the States; and 
(f)   details of allocation made for th is scheme in 2026-27 Budget and whether 
the schemewill be implemented in 16th Finance Commission period? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINIST RY OF HOME AFFAIRS (SHRI NITYANAND 
RAI): (a) to (f) A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a)  The objective of the scheme is to  supplement the efforts of States and 
UTs to strengthen police infrastructure by  equipping the police with the required 
[ 25 March, 2026 ]
73

 
mobility, modern technology, weaponry, communication equipment, forensic set-up, 
etc. and construction including police hous ing in specific areas such as Jammu and 
Kashmir, LWE affected districts and insurgency affected NE States. 
(b)  BE 2025-26 was Rs. 540 crores and RE 2025-26 was Rs. 225 crores.  
(c)  The process involves time intensive preparatory exercise of formulation of 
plans by States/UTs and their approval by this Ministry, followed by procurement 
procedures of tendering and supply completed by States/UTs. Expenditure under the 
scheme is booked upon completion of these processes and receipt of 
equipment/execution of works. Accordin gly, a higher pace of expenditure is 
anticipated towards the latter part of the fi nancial year, (including) i.e by the end of 
March.  
(d)  Expenditure till Feb. 2026 is Rs. 120.68 crore. 
 (e)  This Ministry undertook a comprehensive review during FY 2025-26 and in 
F.Y. 2025-26 (till date), under this Scheme, this Ministry has approved special plans 
of Rs. 2600 crores and sanctioned/released  Rs. 479.34 crore to States/UTs. These 
approvals have resulted in committed liabilities extending beyond FY 2025-26.   
(f) An allocation of ₹450.53 crore has been made for the scheme in the 
Budget 2026–27.

(Amendment) Bill, 2026 and resolves that ten Members of this House be nominated 
by the Chairman to serve on the said Joint Committee." 
The question was put and the motion was adopted. 
 
OBSERVATIONS BY THE CHAIR 
MR. DEPUTY CHAIRMAN : Hon'ble Members, I have received six notices opposing 
the introduction of the Bill on general grounds, inter alia, that it goes against a judicial 
order and also that it is ultra vires the Constitution. Two other notices which were not 
submitted electronically have not been considered.  
Rule 67 of the Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in Council of State 
(Rajya Sabha) provides that if a motion for leave to introduce a Bill is opposed, the 
Chairman, after permitting, if he thinks fi t, a brief explanato ry statement from the 
Member who moves the motion and from the Member who opposes the motion, 
without further debate, put the question be fore the House for a decision. Under this 
rule, a full discussion may be permitted by the Chairman only if the introduction is 
opposed on the ground that the Bill is not within the legislative competence of 
Parliament. 
The power of Parliament to legislate is  only subject to the constitutional 
provisions and not bound by any judicial pronouncements. The subject matter of the 
Bill falls under Entry 2 of List I-Union List of the Seventh Schedule to the Constitution. 
Hence, it is entirely within the legislative competence of Parliament. …(Interruptions)…   
As per earlier rulings of Presiding Officers of this House, the Chair has never 
taken upon itself the responsibility of deciding whether a Bill is unconstitutional or ultra 
vires. It is for the House to either accept or no t accept the Bill. It is the sole privilege 
and duty of the House to deci de every question that aris es on a motion moved by a 
Member. Moreover, all the sections of the House will get ample opportunity to 
present their views either for or against the Bill at the time of its consideration by the 
House.  
I, therefore, do not think it fit to permit full discussion opposing the introduction 
of the Bill. Therefore, now, I would st raight away put the question to the  
House. 
…(Interruptions)…

Constitution.  It deprives the transgender community of the right to freedom, right to 
dignity, right to privacy, right to self-determination. I don't know why the Government 
is attempting to do this.  There are many reasons. I know why th ey have brought the 
Bill.  Even before this Bill was taken up, we discussed the introduction of the CAPF

the judgement and after it dismissed the review petition filed by the Union 
Government, you are again bringing it.  Of course, you have got legislative 
competence. We don't dispute that because  we are also responsible Members of 
Parliament.  But, at the same time, what for is the Legislature? The Parliament or the

sufferings of different sections of the people.

transgenders.  Sir, I am proud to say that I am from the DMK Party, a Party which

Sir, in 2014, I introduced a Bill.  It was debated for two-three Sessions.  So 
many supported it.  Even the Ruling Party su pported me.  I am very proud to say that 
I am a very ordinary person.  I am a Member  of the DMK Party which always brings 
ordinary people to the legislative bodies.  Af ter 45 years since 1970, mine was the first 
Private Member Bill passed in the Rajya Sabha.  Even there was a question on it in the 
UPSC Examination.  Many schools are having  it in the syllabus which is being taught 
to the students.  A Member can bring a Priv ate Member Bill.  In Canada, 249 Private 
Members’ Bills have become Acts.  In UK, 19.  There are many other countries where 
it has happened.  But in India, Private Me mber Bills are struck down.  Thousands of 
Bills have been introduced by various Memb ers.  All have been either defeated or 
forced to be withdrawn taking assurance of  the Government.  But I was very firm in 
my view considering the issues of transg ender community.  My colleague, Shrimati 
Renuka Chowdhury, said that they exist since we exist.  No!  They have been here for 
hundreds of years.  Even when kings were ruling!  They were gu ards for the harems. 
They were with the queens and princesses.  But the society had been treating them in 
such a manner that they were not able to access public places.  Nobody accepted 
them.  They were insulted.  So we thought some dignity must be given to them.  My 
Bill was a comprehensive Bill.  It advocated a national commission to address their 
views; fast courts to hear their cases; and reservation for them in education and 
96
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
employment.   And it was passed here.  Many are not aware of it.  Many know that.  It 
went to the Lok Sabha.  Any Bill which has been passed by either this House or that 
House has to go to the other House or from  there to here.  Would you believe that 
that Bill was debated for nine Sessions?  The Ruling Party, the BJP, which supported 
me here, opposed it there.  Because they wa nted to bring a Bill of their own.  They 
did not want a Private Member to do it.  Th ey held three or four meetings with me 
personally. The Minister for Social Justice and Empowerment called me in his 
Chamber.  We discussed the issue. They took  my views.  I said, ‘You make whatever 
amendments you want to make, but kindly pass this Bill.’   Let there be a legislation 
for them.   The whole transgender comm unity celebrated it across the country and 
across the world.  Who knows Tiruchi Siva in Odisha?  Who knows me in 
Maharashtra?  But they were celebrating it .  They wanted the Bill to be an Act.  
Everyone was appreciating it because such were the provisions.  But the Government 
wanted to supersede that and they wanted to bring a Bill of their own.   
Sir, the problem first came in 2016 when they introduced a Bill.  The definition 
itself created much uproar.  Whenever I go to a college or meet someone, I ask them, 
‘Can you define who is a transgender?’  Sorry to say this, but many here and even 
outside cannot say who a transgender is. They  will say this and they will say that.  
Very clearly, as the court said and as my  Bill said, ‘when the sense of gender a 
person is having does not match with the gender assigned to the person at the time of 
birth.’  You can say that the sense of gender does not match with the gender 
assigned at the time of birth.   At the time  of birth, it would be a male child.  But the 
sense that they will be having is that  they are female.  That person is a  
transgender.  …(Time-bell rings.)…  If you permit, I will speak.  Otherwise, I will sit.

THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. DINESH SHARMA) : The next speaker is, Shri Golla 
Baburao. You have six minutes.  
SHRI GOLLA BABURAO (Andhra Pradesh) : Sir, it is a very sad opportunity to speak 
about transgenders in this supreme House.  As per my knowledge, transgender is the 
most affected social group.  They often f ace social taboos and discrimination.  Their 
life is mysterious and miserable.  We cannot de scribe their difficulties.   I know that in 
many families, those who are confirmed as tr ansgender are simply forced out of their 
families.  This forces many into situatio n of extreme poverty, begging. On the road-
side, they live under inhuman conditions.   
 Sir, I want the Union Government to really send this Bill to the Select 
Committee so that, at least, they can also have a conversation with the stakeholders 
or some Unions or some other important func tionaries in the society so that  this Bill 
can be discussed after  attaining all these things  for the future.  
Sir, in fact, many people are also  mentioning about the Supreme Court 
judgment.  The Supreme Court judgment says th at it is really very, very justiciable for 
the transgender people.  Now I feel, there are more negatives than positives. The 
negative points are that there is a conflic t with NALSA judgment.  The Supreme Court 
in its landmark judgement ‘NALSA versus Unio n of India ruling’ cl early affirmed that 
right to self-identification of gender is a fundamental right under Article 21 of the 
Constitution. The removal of the self-ident ification in the Bill appears to directly 
contradict bridging the judgment.  My part y urges the Government to clarify how this 
provision will survive the constitutional sc rutiny. Sir, there are medical boards. The 
amendment to Section 6, read with the insertion of the term ‘authority’ under Section 
2 making the medical board the authority for identity certification, raises certain 
practical and ethical concerns.  Transgender persons, particularly, those in rural and 
remote areas, may face significant challenges  in  assessing district level facilities, 
appearing before multiple officials, and un dergoing verification procedures.  There is 
also broader concern that identity being a deeply personal matter may be treated as a 
medical issue. Therefore, this approach ma y inadvertently create barriers rather than 
facilitate the case of recognition and ca lls for a more sensitive and accessible 
framework.  
Sir, mandatory reporting just violates pr ivacy.  The insertion of Section 7(1) 
requiring hospitals to report all gender-aff irming surgeries to the Government is a 
violation of the right to privacy as uphe ld in the Puttaswamy Judgment in 2017. 
[ 25 March, 2026 ]
111

 
Medical decisions between a patient and his doctor must remain confidential. This 
provision, if enacted, will expose indi viduals to the risk of harassment and 
surveillance. The YSRCP has historically appro ached the issue of gender identity with 
a degree of caution, favouring the structur ed frameworks to prevent the misuse. We 
are consistent with the view and we are eq ually clear that the safeguards must not 
become barriers but leave genuine persons without legal recognition or State support.  
Sir, my main suggestion is that it needs consideration for a detailed 
parliamentary scrutiny. My number two suggestion is regarding making medical 
evaluation more flexible and sensitized . The conclusion is, India's transgender 
community has waited for a long legal framework that fully protects their rights and 
dignity.  The 2019 Act was an important step forward even if it ha d certain limitations. 
However, the present amendment has, in some  respects, given rise to concerns that 
progress achieved may not be fully sustai ned. I again humbly request the hon’ble 
Government to revisit certain provisions that may not be fully aligned with the 
constitutional values of dignity, equality and individual autonomy. So this may be 
referred to the Select Committee for further deliberation. Thank you, Sir.

protect welfare of transgenders.  The Expert Committee on transgender issue was 
constituted only in January 2014 in the dying weeks of the UPA Government.  Even 
after a decade of ruling the country, the Co ngress did nothing as promised in their 
manifesto.  But, only the Modi Government  took the effort to bring this Bill and 
protect the rights of transgenders.  Ther efore, why has Shri Tiruchi Shiva gone on 
accusing the NDA Government for the welfar e measures, which they failed to bring 
during the UPA Government period?  I want to know that.   
At the same time, I appreciate Modi ji, who has not only brought the Bill, but 
now, he has also introduced certain clause s which protect their rights.  That is the 
important thing.  That is the way to do transgender welfare.   Their Members are 
opposing the Bill.  They have the right to oppose.  But, why did Congress and other 
parties, like DMK, fail during that period in bringing any Bill of this kind to protect 
transgenders’ rights?  That is what I want  to know. Apart from  that, there are so

introduced that.  But I want to sa
y certain things in addition.   I would like to say that

and skill development for transgender persons.   That is very important.  Secondly,

THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. DINESH SHARMA):  Now, Shri Ravi Chandra Vaddiraju.  
SHRI RAVI CHANDRA VADDIRAJU (Telangana) :  Sir, today, I stand before you to 
speak about an important issue of transgender rights.  This  is not only about law or 
policy.  It is about respect, dignity and equa l rights for every human being.  India has 
a long history of recognising different gender identities.  Communities like hijras and 
kinnars have been part of our culture fo r centuries.  But even today, many 
transgender persons face discrimination, re jection and hardship in daily life. To 
address this, the Government brought th e Transgender Persons (Protection of 
Rights) Act, 2019. This law was a major step. It aimed to give legal recognition and 
protect the rights of transgender pers ons.  Now, the Transgender Persons 
(Protection of Rights) Amendment Bill, 2026  has been introduced.  This Bill makes 
some important changes.  Some of these changes are positive, but some also raise 
concerns.   
First, let us look at the definition of a transgender person. The earlier law had a 
broad and inclusive definition.  But the ne w amendment removes that definition and 
lists only certain categories. It also ex cludes some identities based on self-
perception. This brings up an important qu estion -Should identity be decided by the 
individual or by the system?  Identity is personal and any law should respect that.   
Next is the process of identity certificatio n. Under the new Bill, a transgender person 
must apply to the District Magistrate. The certificate will be issued based on a Medical 
Board's recommendation.  While this may bring up a structure, it also creates 
difficulties. It adds medical and administra tive steps in a very personal matter. Many 
believe that self-identification should be enough. This Bill also makes it compulsory to 
get a revised certificate after gender chan ge surgery. It also requires medical 
[ 25 March, 2026 ]
117

 
institutions to report such cases. This raises concerns about privacy and unnecessary 
procedures.   
At the same time, the Bill has taken stro ng steps to punish serious crimes. It 
introduces strict punishment for forcin g someone to become transgender through 
violence, kidnapping or causing serious harm and also for forcing transgender 
persons into begging or bonded labour.  These offences now have strict penalties 
including long-term imprisonment and heavy fine s.  This is a positive step.  It shows 
that the law recognizes the seriousness of such crimes.  
From Telangana perspective, there ha ve been some constructive policy 
efforts. The Government led by Shri K. Chandrashekar Rao introduced welfare 
measures like monthly pensions, financial assistance and took steps towards social 
rec ognition of transgender persons.  …( Time bell rings)… Telangana has also 
included transgender persons in some welf are schemes.  However, challenges still 
remain.  Many people in the transgender community continue to face problems in 
employment, healthcare and education. Social discrimination is still a big issue. 
Therefore, there is a need to improve implementation, provide skill development, and 
create more awareness in society.  
THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. DINESH SHARMA): Please conclude. 
SHRI RAVI CHANDRA VADDIRAJU: Sir, I will take only thirty seconds more.  Sir, laws 
alone cannot change society. Real change  comes when people's attitudes change. 
Transgender persons should not have to struggle for basic rights like education, 
employment and healthcare.  We must pr ovide them equal opportunities in jobs, 
access to proper healthcare as well as inclusion in schools and colleges.  Most 
importantly, we should ensure that they get respect in society.  
उपसभाध्यक्ष(डा. िदनेश शमार्): आपकी बात आ गई। 
SHRI RAVI CHANDRA VADDIRAJU : This Amendment Bill is an  important step but we 
must make sure that it remains inclusive and fair. Policies should empower people, 
not create more barriers.   
THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. DINESH SHARMA): Next speaker is Dr. John Brittas.

build a society where everyone is treated equally, with dignity and respect irrespective

MS. SWATI MALIWAL (National Capital Territory of Delhi) : Sir, I rise today to place 
before this House the weight of lives that cannot be ignored.  Sir, consider the case of 
Jane Kaushik, a qualified transgender teac her who secured jobs in two States, yet 
was forced to resign, not because of lack of merit but because of her identity.  In 
2023, Pranshu, a 16-year-old trans-artist from Ujjain, committed suicide because she 
faced relentless online hate.  These are not isolated stories.  Discrimination and abuse 
remain the lived reality of thousands and lakhs of transgenders for centuries. Even 
today, Transgender Welfare Boards are absent in 11 States and UTs. The National 
Council for Transgenders has met only a handful number of times. Sir, Garima Grehs, 
shelters for abandoned transgender person s are only 23 across 17 States. The 2019 
law provides for sex reassignment surger ies, but how many Government hospitals 
actually provide it? And, while the Act pr ohibits discrimination, it provides no 
penalties.  Sir, a right without consequence is not a right, it is merely a suggestion.    
Sir, in the historic NALSA judgment of  2014, the Supreme Court affirmed that 
gender identity is a fundamental right - no surgery, no certificate, no State approval. 
The 2019 Act recognized this, but the pres ent Amendment Bill narrows the definition 
of the transgender persons. It excludes identities based on self-perception and 
mandates medical certification.  It replace s dignity with diagnosis, identity with 
inspection.  Sir, no other citizen in this co untry is required to prove who they are in 
this manner.   
From my experience in the Delhi Commi ssion for Women, I have seen how 
certification becomes a process of humili ation. Though the law mandates that 
transgender certificates shall be made in 30  days, delays often stretch to months and 
even years
. Officials often as k degrading questions like ‘ आप मीठे हो’ ? Some even 
said, ‘िक आपको देखने से नहीं लगता िक आप टर्ांस हो’।   Now we are adding another layer 
of verification. This is not correct. Further, under the 2019 Act and even the 
Amendment, sexual abuse of a transgender person carries a punishment of only six

MESSAGE FROM LOK SABHA 
The Finance Bill, 2026 
SECRETARY-GENERAL: Sir, with your kind permission, I rise to report that the Lok 
Sabha at its sitting held on 25 th March, 2026, passed the Finance Bill, 2026. The 
Speaker has certified that the Bill is a Mone y Bill within the meanin g of Article 110 of 
the Constitution. 
 I lay a copy of the said Bill on the Table.
 
GOVERNMENT BILLS 
The Central Armed Police Forces (General Administration) Bill, 2026-Contd 
THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. M. THAMBIDURAI):  Please continue.  
SHRI GOLLA BABURAO: Sir, this Bill, therefore, is not just an administrative reform. It 
is a structural intervention that seeks to rede fine the functioning, status and future of 
the Central Armed Police Forces. The overr eaching vision of strengthening of our 
internal security is appreciated, but we mu st ensure that this legislative framework 
translates into dignity and parity for those on front lines.   
Sir, the important feature of this Bill is  recognition of specialization within the 
Central Armed Police Polices. While it is of ten argued that Officers from IPS bring 
administrative and policy expertise, it is equally true that CAPF personnel develop 
deep domain expertise through years of rigorous field experience -- whether in high-
attitude warfare or survival in ITBP or counter-insurgency operations in CISF or border 
intelligence and surveillance in BSF.  
Mr. Vice-Chairman, Sir, for the first time since Independence, the single 
comprehensive legislation codifies and unifies the administrative framework governing 
all the five major CAPFs, replacing the patchwork of separate  rules, excessive 
instructions and ad hoc practices with clear and transparent statutory provisions. 
Recruitment, promotions, cadre review, se rvice conditions, disciplinary procedures 
and grievance redressal – all brought under one legal umbrella. We acknowledge that 
this is a genuine and necessary reform.  
Sir, the proposed Bill is expected to  enhance the administrative clarity and 
operational efficiency within the Forces. By  harmonizing service rules and addressing 
longstanding grievances, it aims to improve the morale and effectiveness. Before this 
162
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
Bill, the absence of a unified framework re sulted in administrative challenges and 
frequent litigation on service related issues , creating uncertainty fo r 10 lakh personnel 
and their families about their career path and entitlements.  A law that replaces 
litigation with clarity is a law that serves our soldiers well.  
Sir, some associations of former CAPF  personnel have specifically requested 
that this Bill be sent to the Parliamentary Standing Committee on Home Affairs for 
wider consultation and that CAPF cadre offi cers be included in giving suggestions on 
the Bill.  A legislation of this magnitude – one that affects the career and dignity of ten 
lakh personnel – deserves the most thorough  parliamentary scrutiny.  We urge the 
Government to look into this request.  A stronger consultation process will produce a 
stronger law.   
 Sir, Andhra Pradesh has given this na tion thousands of brave CAPF officers 
and jawans.  Their operational experience – on the Maoist corridor, on the Himalayan 
border, on election duty in volatile districts – is invaluable institutional knowledge.  
We recommend the creation of a formal st atutory advisory body comprising senior 
CAPF cadre officers to contribute to policy formulation.      
 Further, there is a crisis within the CAPF , that this Bill must address and that is 
the devastating toll of suicides among ou r personnel.  From 2020 to 2024, 730 CAPF 
personnel have died by suicide – 134 in 2024 alone.  In the same period (2020 to 
2024), 47,891 personnel opted for voluntary retirement and 7,664 resigned.   
Between 2011 and 2021, the suicide rate in  C A P F s  s t o o d  a t  1 4  p e r  l a k h  p e r s o n n e l  
compared to 11 per lakh at the all-India level. 
 Sir, we strongly recommend that this Bill include a mandatory, statutory mental 
health framework for all CAPF personnel, namely,   mandatory confidential 
psychological screening for all personnel re turning from operational deployments; a 
24/7 anonymous mental health helpline – similar to CISF’s Project – be made 
statutory and extended across all five CAPF cadre officers; a minimum guaranteed 
leave of 100 days per year – codified in th e Bill – so that personnel can meaningfully 
address family pressures befo re they become crises; and removal of stigma around 
mental health by ensuring that seeking psychological help will not affect promotions or 
service records. 
 Sir, the men and women of our Cent ral Armed Police Forces seek only  
fairness – a clear career path, adequate leav e, a secure pension, and recognition of

              Shrimati Sudha Murty- ‘Need for a nati onal-level scientific probe and 
awareness campaign on the safe use of weight-loss drugs.’                     
Need for a national-level scientific probe and awareness campaign on  
the safe use of weight-loss drugs 
SHRIMATI SUDHA MURTY (Nominated) : Sir, thank you for giving an opportunity to 
speak. My subject is, ‘Need for a national-level scientific probe and awareness 
campaign on the safe use of weight-loss drugs.’  Sir,  I seek to bring to the attention 
of the Government a new development in our healthcare sector. In March, 2026, the 
patents for popular diabetic drugs also used for weight loss, like Ozempic and 
Mounjaro expired in India. Many Indian pharmaceutical  companies have already 
entered the market to produce these me dicines. As a result, these drugs are 
becoming significantly cheaper, with some no w available at nearl
y 50 per cent of their 
previous price. While it is good that medici ne is becoming affordable, we must also 
be careful. In a price-sensitive market lik e India, the sudden mass availability could 
lead to widespread misuse for weight lo ss without adequate medical supervision. 
Clinical studies across the world have linked these medications to side effects ranging 
from gastrointestinal distress to rarer, seve re conditions like pancreatitis, gallbladder 
166
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
issues and potential muscle loss. I reques t that the Government, through the ICMR 
(Indian Council of Medical Re search) and CDSCO (Central Drugs Standard Control 
Organisation) initiate a formal and large-scale scientific probe to monitor the long-
term impact of these drugs on the Indian po pulation. We should lead a strong public 
awareness campaign to educate our citizens . People need to know that these are 
medical treatments, not simple lifestyle prod ucts. I also request that the Government 
issues clear safety guidelines for healthcar e providers. Our goal must be to ensure 
that these new medical breakthroughs he lp improve lives without causing any 
unintended health problems.

              Shri Kesridevsinh Jhala; need for strengthening ban on single-use plastics 
through integrated infrastructure reuse. 
 
Need for strengthening the ban on single-use plastics through integrated 
infrastructure reuse 
SHRI KESRIDEVSINH JHALA (Gujarat) : Sir, I draw the attention of the Government 
to the serious menace of plastic pollu tion spreading across our nation. The 
uncontrolled infiltration of mi croplastics into our food, water, air, and soil is deeply 
concerning. This crisis impacts our cattle, wildlife, and marine ecosystems, with over 
267 species already affected. This silent threat jeopardizes our public health, 
biodiversity, and long-term sustainability.  Under the visionary leadership of the 
hon’ble Prime Minister, India has taken pr oactive strides banning 19 categories of 
single-use plastics and achieving a recyclin g rate well above the global average. Yet, 
challenges remain as mismanaged waste cont inues to impact our landfills and rivers. 
We must now move toward a paradigm of  total integration, where aggressive 
reduction and innovative reuse go hand in hand.  I urge the Government to mandate 
Corporate Social Responsibility contributions from e-commerce and corporate giants 
for plastic recovery, while strictly enforc ing extended producer responsibility. We 
must scale the Central Institute of Petroc hemicals Engineering and  Technology to 
train our MSMEs in manufacturing eco-alternatives like bio-bags, creating sustainable 
[ 25 March, 2026 ]
167

livelihoods. Most importantly, the plastic we cannot eliminate, must be transformed 
from a liability into a national asset. I prop ose a mandate to integrate collected plastic 
waste into road construction and infrastruc ture projects. Plastic-bitumen blends offer 
superior durability and water resistance, turning environmental waste into the very 
foundation of our highways.  Decisive action today will ensure a cleaner, greener, and 
plastic-smart India tomorrow. Thank you, Sir.

Demand for re-establishment, operationalization and upgradation of

SHRI NAGENDRA RAY (West Bengal) :  S i r ,  I  s e e k  t o  d r a w  a t t e n t i o n  o f  t h e  
Government towards the need for re-estab lishment and operationalization of Cooch 
Behar Heritage Airport and its upgradation into a dual-use civil and military airbase, in 
the larger interest of regional connect ivity and economic development defending 
national security from any hostile countries like China, Pakistan, Bangladesh and 
other countries for religious terrorist activit ies.  It is a known fact that China and 
Bangladesh are targeting Siliguri Corridor as the “eye of a bird”  from the Lalmonirhat 
Military airbase in Bangladesh to discon nect North-East from our Country.  The 
Cooch Behar Heritage Airport holds historical  significance and is strategically located 
168
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
in North Bengal, close to international bord ers. This airport had an outstanding role 
during 1st and 2 nd World Wars in 1914 and 1939.  Given its geographical importance, 
revival and development of this airport would greatly strengthen air connectivity in this 
region.  Re-establishing civil aviation operations from Cooch Behar would significantly 
improve regional air connectivity, promote tourism, facilitate business activities, and 
provide faster access to medical and em ergency services.  At the same time, 
operationalization of the airport as a military airbase would enhance strategic 
preparedness and logistical capability in this sensitive border region, complementing 
national defence objectives. I also request that the airport be upgraded with modern 
aviation infrastructure, navigational and communication systems, safety and security 
installations, and supporting road and ut ility connectivity.  Development of allied 
infrastructure would also generate empl oyment opportunities and catalyze socio-
